Spectral Estimators for Structured Generalized Linear Models via Approximate Message Passing
Yihan Zhang, Hong Chang Ji, Ramji Venkataramanan, Marco Mondelli

Abstract
We consider the problem of parameter estimation in a high-dimensional generalized linear model. Spectral methods obtained via the principal eigenvector of a suitable data-dependent matrix provide a simple yet surprisingly effective solution. However, despite their wide use, a rigorous performance characterization, as well as a principled way to preprocess the data, are available only for unstructured (i.i.d. Gaussian and Haar orthogonal) designs. In contrast, real-world data matrices are highly structured and exhibit non-trivial correlations. To address the problem, we consider correlated Gaussian designs capturing the anisotropic nature of the features via a covariance matrix Σ \Sigma . Our main result is a precise asymptotic characterization of the performance of spectral estimators. This allows us to identify the optimal preprocessing that minimizes the number of samples needed for parameter estimation. Surprisingly, such preprocessing is universal across a broad set of designs, which partly addresses a conjecture on optimal spectral estimators for rotationally invariant models. Our principled approach vastly improves upon previous heuristic methods, including for designs common in computational imaging and genetics. The proposed methodology, based on approximate message passing, is broadly applicable and opens the way to the precise characterization of spiked matrices and of the corresponding spectral methods in a variety of settings.



1
Introduction

This paper considers the prototypical problem of learning a parameter vector from observations obtained via a generalized linear model (GLM)
[
MN89
]
:

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=q\left(\left\langle x_{i},\beta^{*}\right\rangle,\varepsilon_{i}\right),\quad 1\leq i\leq n,

(1.1)

where
\beta^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
consists of (unknown) regression coefficients.
The statistician wishes to estimate
\beta^{*}
based on the observations
y=(y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and the covariate vectors
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. The vector
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
contains (unknown) i.i.d. random variables accounting for noise in the measurements.
The (known) link function
q\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is applied element-wise, i.e.,
q(g,\varepsilon)=(q(g_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\cdots,q(g_{n},\varepsilon_{n}))
for any
g,\varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
The nonlinearity
q
generalizes linear regression (
q(g,\varepsilon)=g+\varepsilon
) and incorporates various problems in statistics, machine learning, signal processing and computational biology, e.g.,
phase retrieval (
q(g,\varepsilon)=|g|+\varepsilon
)
[
FS20
]
,
1
-bit compressed sensing (
q(g,\varepsilon)=\sgn(g)+\varepsilon
)
[
BB08
]
, and logistic regression
[
SC19
]
.

For estimation in GLMs, several works have considered methods based on convex programming, e.g.
[
CSV13
,
WdM15
,
TR19
]
.
However, these methods often become computationally infeasible as
d
grows.
Thus, fast iterative methods including
alternating minimization
[
NJS15
]
, approximate message passing
[
Ran11
]
, Wirtinger flow
[
CLS15b
]
, iterative projections
[
LGL15
]
, and the Kaczmarz method
[
Wei15
]
has been developed.
Due to their iterative nature, to converge to an informative solution, these procedures require a “warm start”, i.e., a vector
\widehat{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
whose “overlap”
|\langle{\widehat{\beta}},{\beta^{*}}\rangle|/(\|\widehat{\beta}\|_{2}\|\beta^{*}\|_{2})

with
\beta^{*}
is non-vanishing for large
d
. In this paper, we focus on
spectral estimators

[
CCFM21
]
, which provide a simple yet effective approach for estimating
\beta^{*}
, and serve as a warm start for the local methods above.
Spectral estimators have been applied in a range of problems including
polynomial learning
[
CM20
]
,
estimation from mixed linear regression
[
YCS14
]
and
ranking
[
CFMW19
]
.
For the GLM in
Equation

1.1
, the spectral estimator processes the observations via a function
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and outputs the principal eigenvector of the matrix

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}.

(1.2)

To understand the accuracy of spectral estimators, it is crucial to:

(i)
characterize their performance (e.g., in terms of limiting overlap), and
(ii)
design the preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}
that minimizes the sample complexity, i.e., the number
n
of observations required to attain a desired limiting overlap.
This work gives precise answers to both these questions, providing solid performance guarantees as well as a principled basis for optimizing spectral estimators used in practical applications.

A line of work
[
NJS15
,
CLS15b
,
CC17
]

has bounded the sample complexity of spectral estimators obtained from
Equation

1.2

for i.i.d. Gaussian designs via matrix concentration inequalities. However, these bounds require the number
n
of observations to substantially exceed the parameter dimension
d
, and they
are not sharp enough to optimize
\mathcal{T}
.
Using tools from random matrix theory, the works
[
LL20
,
MM19
]
obtained tight results in the proportional regime where
n,d\to\infty
and
n/d\to\delta
for a fixed constant
\delta\in(0,\infty)
(called the “aspect ratio”). Specifically, a
phase transition
phenomenon is established: if
\delta
exceeds a critical value (referred to as the “spectral threshold”), then
(i)
a spectral gap emerges between the first two eigenvalues of
D
, and
(ii)
the spectral estimator attains non-vanishing correlation with
\beta^{*}
. For
\delta
below this critical value, there is no outlier to the right of the spectrum of
D
, and the spectral estimator is asymptotically independent of
\beta^{*}
.
This precise characterization
allows to derive the optimal preprocessing function that minimizes the spectral threshold
[
MM19
]
and also that maximizes the overlap for a given
\delta

[
LAL19
]
. These results are extended by
[
DBMM20
,
MDX+21
]
to cover a sub-sampled Haar design, consisting of a subset of columns from a uniformly random orthogonal matrix.

The line of work above crucially relies on the design matrix
X=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1},&\cdots,&x_{n}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
being unstructured, namely
i.i.d. Gaussian or rotationally-invariant with unit singular values. In contrast, design matrices occurring in practice are highly structured and their entries exhibit significant correlations (e.g., in computational genomics
[
LTS+13
]
and imaging
[
CLS15a
]
). In this paper, we capture the correlation and heterogeneity of the data via general (
correlated
) Gaussian designs.
Specifically, each covariate
x_{i}
is an i.i.d.
d
-dimensional
zero-mean Gaussian vector with an arbitrary positive definite covariance matrix
\Sigma/n\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
. The covariance matrix
\Sigma
captures correlations between covariates and the heterogeneity in their variances.
General Gaussian designs (e.g., with Toeplitz or circulant covariance structures) have been widely adopted in high-dimensional regression models
[
JM14b
,
JM14a
,
JM18
,
ZZ14
,
vdGBRD14
,
Wai09
]
. However, existing results largely focus on (penalized) maximum-likelihood estimators for linear and logistic models

[
CM21
,
CMW23
,
SC19
,
ZSC22
,
Sur19
]
.
An asymptotic theory of spectral estimators for GLMs with general Gaussian designs has been lacking. One significant challenge is that current techniques for i.i.d. and Haar designs all crucially depend on their right rotational invariance, which fails to hold for correlated covariates.

1.1
Main results

Our main contribution is to give
a
precise asymptotic characterization of the overlap between the leading eigenvector of
D
and the unknown parameter

\beta^{*}
, as well as the locations of the top two eigenvalues of
D
, provided a criticality condition holds. This is the content of
Theorem

3.1
, which is informally stated below.

Theorem

(Informal version of
Theorem

3.1
)
.

Consider the GLM in
Equation

1.1
under a general Gaussian design with covariance
\Sigma/n
. Assume
n,d\to\infty
with
n/d\to\delta\in(0,\infty)
.
Let
\overline{\Sigma}
be a random variable whose law is the limiting eigenvalue distribution of
\Sigma
.
Fix
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and let
\beta^{\mathrm{spec}}
denote the leading eigenvector of the matrix
D
defined in
Equation

1.2
.
Then, there exist computable scalars
F(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}}),\lambda_{1}(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}}),\lambda_{2}(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}}),\eta(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})
such that the following holds.
If
F(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})>0
, then:

1.

The limits of the top two eigenvalues of
D
equal
\lambda_{1}(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})>\lambda_{2}(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})
, respectively; and

2.

\frac{\left|\left\langle\beta^{\mathrm{spec}},\beta^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|\beta^{\mathrm{spec}}\right\|_{2}\left\|\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}}\to\eta(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})>0
.

The performance characterization of spectral estimators provided by our main result opens the way to their principled optimization. In
Section

3.1
, we optimize the preprocessing
\mathcal{T}
towards minimizing the spectral threshold. A remarkable feature of the optimal preprocessing is that it depends on the covariance matrix
\Sigma
of the design only through its normalized trace.
In other words, it is
universally optimal over any covariance structure
with fixed trace.
An important practical implication is that to apply the optimal spectral estimator, only the normalized trace
\frac{1}{d}\tr(\Sigma)
needs to be estimated, instead of the whole matrix
\Sigma
.
In the proportional regime, the scalar
\frac{1}{d}\tr(\Sigma)
can be estimated consistently using a simple plug-in estimator involving the sample covariance matrix. In contrast, consistent estimation of
\Sigma
typically requires a sample size larger than that needed by the spectral estimator itself,
see
Remark

3.5
for details. Our result on the optimal spectral threshold also resolves in part a conjecture in
[
MKLZ22
]
on optimal spectral methods for rotationally invariant designs; see
Section

3.2
.

The criticality condition
F(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})>0
does not depend on the data and can be easily checked numerically.
Whenever the condition holds, our results imply that

(i)
the top eigenvalue is detached from the bulk of the spectrum of
D
, hence constituting an outlier, and

(ii)
the spectral estimator attains strictly positive asymptotic overlap.
We conjecture that
F(\delta,\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}})>0
is in fact necessary to achieve positive overlap, see
Remark

3.4
.

1.2
Technical ideas

Our goal is to characterize top eigenvector and top two eigenvalues of the matrix
D
in
Equation

1.2
, which can be expressed as
X^{\top}TX
, with
X=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1},&\cdots,&x_{n}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
and
T=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(y))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
. From the analysis for i.i.d. Gaussian designs
[
LL20
,
MM19
]
, we expect that the dependence between
T
and
X
will, under a suitable criticality condition, lead to an outlier eigenvalue in the spectrum of
D
, and when this happens, the corresponding eigenvector (i.e., the spectral estimator) has non-zero overlap with
\beta^{*}
. Note that

\displaystyle D=X^{\top}TX=\Sigma^{1/2}\widetilde{X}^{\top}T\widetilde{X}\Sigma^{1/2},

(1.3)

where
\widetilde{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
has i.i.d.
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/n)
entries. If
T
were independent of
X
, then
D
would be a
spiked separable covariance matrix
recently studied in
[
DY21
]
. However, in the GLM setting,
y
(and, thus,
T
) depends on
X
via the 1-dimensional projection
X\beta^{*}
, so results from
[
DY21
]
cannot be applied. Indeed, to the best of our knowledge, there is no off-the-shelf result in random matrix theory giving spectral information on
D
. Existing techniques for i.i.d. Gaussian designs
[
LL20
,
MM19
]
also seem difficult to extrapolate as
X
is not isotropic.

To overcome these difficulties, we propose a novel proof strategy using the theory of approximate message passing (AMP). Specifically, AMP refers to a family of iterative algorithms that are specified by a sequence of ‘denoising’ functions.
A key feature of AMP is the presence of a memory term, which debiases the iterates, ensuring that their joint empirical distribution is asymptotically Gaussian. This in turn allows to track their covariance structure via a low-dimensional recursion known as
state evolution

[
BM11
,
Bol14
]
. Our key idea is to simulate a power iteration using AMP: via a judicious choice of denoisers, we ensure that the AMP recursion, once executed for a sufficiently large number of steps, approximates an eigenequation of
D
. Then, we leverage state evolution to:

•

identify the location of the outliers in the spectrum of
D
, by controlling the
\ell_{2}
-norm of the iterates of AMP, and

•

establish the limiting correlation between the top eigenvector of
D
and
\beta^{*}
, by characterizing the inner product of the iterates with the parameter vector
\beta^{*}
.

The idea of using AMP to simulate an algorithm whose output is aligned with the estimator of interest has been used to characterize the asymptotic performance in many settings
[
DM16
,
BKRS21
,
BKRS23
,
Rus20
,
LW21
,
SC19
]
. We highlight that,
for the study of spectral estimators for GLMs, all previous works using AMP as a proof technique
[
MTV21
,
MV22
,
ZMV22
]
require precise knowledge of when a spectral gap emerges. For the settings considered in those works, complete characterizations of the spectrum are available via known results from random matrix theory. This is however not the case for our setting with a correlated Gaussian design.
In this work, we exploit random matrix theory tools for studying the right edge of the bulk.
The fundamental novelty of our approach is that the more challenging task of locating the spike is accomplished by AMP.

1.3
Related work

Spectral methods.

Spectral methods find applications in various domains across statistics and data science
[
CCFM21
]
and, as discussed earlier,
the spectrally-initialized optimization paradigm is widely employed for estimation from GLMs and their variants.
Beyond GLMs, other applications include
community detection
[
Abb17
]
,
clustering
[
NJW01
]
, angular synchronization in cryo-EM
[
Sin11
]
, inference of low-rank matrices
[
MV21
]
and tensors
[
RM14
]
.

Approximate message passing.

Approximate message passing algorithms were first proposed for linear regression
[
Kab03
,
DMM09
,
KMS+12
]
, and have since been applied to several statistical estimation
problems, including parameter recovery in a GLM
[
BKM+19
,
Ran11
,
SC19
,
RSF19
,
VKM22
]
; see the review
[
FVRS22
]
and references therein. In this paper, AMP is used solely as a tool for analyzing spectral estimators.
Following
[
MV22
,
CMW20
,
MW22
]
, we expect that our results can be used to analyze general first order iterative methods (including AMP itself) with spectral initialization.
An alternative way to initialize first order methods is via random initialization.
A recent line of work
[
LW22
,
LFW23
]
analyzes AMP with spectral and random initializations in the context of symmetric rank-
1
matrix estimation, by
establishing a non-asymptotic state evolution result. A different non-asymptotic analysis of AMP, leveraging a leave-one-out approach, was recently put forward in
[
BHX23
]
.

Random matrix theory.

The separable covariance matrix model
[
PS09
,
CH14
,
Yan19
]
and its spiked counterpart
[
DY21
,
DY22
]
are related to the matrix
D
that we study, but as discussed earlier, the results in these papers cannot be applied to GLMs with correlated designs. A related (and more general) model is considered in
[
LM21
]
, where potential outlier eigenvalues/eigenvectors are identified via a deterministic equivalent of the resolvent.
However,
[
LM21
]
provides no explicit condition under which these outliers indeed emerge.
In comparison, our result locates both the right edge of the spectral bulk and the outlier eigenvalue, yielding an almost sure characterization.
Our approach has the advantage of rendering itself ready for initializing iterative procedures.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Generalized linear models with general Gaussian designs

Recall that the goal is to estimate the parameter vector
\beta^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
from observations obtained via the model in
Equation

1.1
. We write
y=q(X\beta^{*},\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for the observation vector, with the link function
q
acting component-wise on its inputs. We make the following assumptions on the model:

(A1)

\beta^{*}\sim P^{\otimes d}
,
1
1

1

For a tuple of distributions
P_{1},\cdots,P_{k}
,
P_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes P_{k}
denotes the product distribution with
P_{i}
being its
i
-th marginal.
If all
P_{i}
’s are equal to
P
, we use the notation
P^{\otimes k}
.
where
P
is a distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}
with mean
0
and variance
1
.

(A2)

For
1\leq i\leq n
,
x_{i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0_{d},\Sigma/n)
independent of
\beta^{*}
, where
\Sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is deterministic and strictly positive definite with empirical spectral distribution
2
2

2

The empirical spectral distribution of a
p\times p
matrix is a probability measure that assigns weight
1/p
to a Dirac mass supported at each of the eigenvalues.

converging weakly to the law of a random variable
\overline{\Sigma}
compactly supported on
(0,\infty)
.
The spectral norm
\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}
is uniformly bounded over
d
and,
for all
\varsigma>0
, there exists
d_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
,

\displaystyle\supp(\mu_{\Sigma})\subset\supp(\overline{\mu}_{\Sigma})+[-\varsigma,\varsigma],

(2.1)

where
\mu_{\Sigma}
and
\overline{\mu}_{\Sigma}
denote respectively the empirical and limiting spectral distributions of
\Sigma
,
\supp
denotes their support and ‘
+
’ denotes the Minkowski sum.

(A3)

\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is independent of
(\beta^{*},X)
and has empirical distribution converging in probability in Wasserstein-
2
distance to
P_{\varepsilon}
which is a distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}
with bounded second moment.

(A4)

We work in the proportional regime where
n,d\to\infty
with
n/d\to\delta
for some
\delta\in(0,\infty)
.

Item

(A1)
specifies an i.i.d. prior distribution on the unknown parameter
\beta^{*}
.
We remark that our analysis carries over to
\beta^{*}\sim\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
(where
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
denotes the unit sphere in dimension
d
), giving
the same results as for
P={\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
Spectral estimators are unable to exploit any prior structure in the parameter vector, since the eigenvectors of the spectral matrix are not a priori guaranteed to obey structures (e.g., binary, sparse or conic) that may be enjoyed by the parameter.
In fact, our results are universal with respect to
P
.
We leave it for future work to perform parameter estimation with prior information taken into account.

The
general
Gaussian design in
Item

(A2)
constitutes the major challenge of this work. We highlight that no distributional assumption is imposed on the matrix
\Sigma
:
this in particular means that
X
is only
left
rotationally invariant in law.
As such, the model falls out of the bi-rotationally invariant ensemble which has recently attracted a flurry of research
[
Fan22
,
VKM22
,
WZF22
,
MKLZ22
,
CR23
]
.
The requirement of strict positive definiteness of
\Sigma
could be relaxed to positive semidefiniteness with the modification in the proof that

\Sigma^{-1}
is replaced with the pseudoinverse
\Sigma^{+}
and
\overline{\Sigma}
is replaced with a proper mixture of
\delta_{0}
(where
\delta_{\lambda}
is the Dirac delta measure at
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
) and a certain absolutely continuous (with respect to the Lebesgue measure) probability measure.
The assumption on uniform boundedness of
\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}
is technical and is satisfied by many natural covariance structures used in practice, such as Toeplitz or circulant. The condition
Equation

2.1
excludes outlier eigenvalues from the spectrum of
\Sigma
.
Otherwise, it is known that spikes in
\Sigma
will result in spikes in
D

[
DY21
,
BBCF17
,
DJ23
]
. These additional spikes are undesirable from an inference perspective, as they may be confused with the one contributed by the unknown parameter
\beta^{*}
.

The proportionality between parameter dimension
d
and sample size
n
in
Item

(A4)
is a natural scaling since the spectral estimator starts being correlated with
\beta^{*}
in this regime.

2.2
Spectral estimator

The spectral estimator is defined as

\displaystyle\beta^{\mathrm{spec}}(y,X)

\displaystyle\coloneqq v_{1}\left(D\right)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1},

(2.2)

where
v_{1}(\cdot)
denotes the principal eigenvector.
We also define random variables

\displaystyle(\overline{G},\overline{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(0,\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]\right)\otimes P_{\varepsilon},\quad\overline{Y}=q(\overline{G},\overline{\varepsilon}).

(2.3)

We make the following assumption on the preprocessing function:

(A5)

{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is bounded and satisfies:

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\supp(\overline{Y})}{\mathcal{T}}(y)>0.

(2.4)

Furthermore,
{\mathcal{T}}
is pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order, i.e., there exist
j
and
L
such that

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{T}}(x)-{\mathcal{T}}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq L\left|x-y\right|\left(1+\left|x\right|^{j-1}+\left|y\right|^{j-1}\right),\qquad\mbox{for all }x,y.

(2.5)

The condition in
Equation

2.4
is rather mild: it is satisfied by the optimal preprocessing function (see
Theorem

3.2
), and it is also required by prior work for
\Sigma=I_{d}

[
MM19
,
LAL19
]
.

Finally, we single out two technical conditions that guarantee the well-posedness of the auxiliary quantities appearing in the statement of our main result,
Theorem

3.1
.

(A6)

For any
x\neq 0
, let

\displaystyle s\coloneqq\begin{cases}x\cdot(\sup\supp(\overline{\Sigma})),&x>0\\
x\cdot(\inf\supp(\overline{\Sigma})),&x<0\end{cases},

where we use
\supp(\overline{\Sigma})
to denote the support of the density function of
\overline{\Sigma}
.
Then for any
x\neq 0
, the random variable
\overline{\Sigma}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\searrow s}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\gamma-x\overline{\Sigma}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\lim_{\gamma\searrow s}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\left(\gamma-x\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\lim_{\gamma\searrow s}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{3}}{\left(\gamma-x\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\infty.

(2.6)

(A7)

The function
{\mathcal{T}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{a\searrow\,\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\lim_{a\searrow\,\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{G}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\infty.

(2.7)

We remark that these two conditions can be removed, at the price of a slightly more involved definition of such auxiliary quantities; see
Remark

3.3
.

3
Main results

Our main contribution,
Theorem

3.1
, gives a precise asymptotic characterization of the overlap between the leading eigenvector of
D
and the unknown parameter, provided a criticality condition is satisfied. This condition ensures that
D
has a spectral gap in the high-dimensional limit.
Theorem

3.1
also gives asymptotic formulas for the location of the right edge of the bulk and for the (right) outlier eigenvalue of
D
.

To state the results, we require some definitions.
For
a\in(\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})),\infty)
, let

\displaystyle s(a)\coloneqq\begin{cases}(\sup\supp(\overline{\Sigma}))\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right],&\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]>0\\
(\inf\supp(\overline{\Sigma}))\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right],&\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]<0\\
0,&\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]=0\end{cases}.

(3.1)

Note that
s(a)
also depends on
\overline{\Sigma}
and
{\mathcal{T}}
.
For
a>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))
, define the function

\displaystyle\varphi(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{G}^{2}{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\gamma(a)-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right],\qquad\psi(a)=a\gamma(a),

(3.2)

where
\gamma(a)
is an implicit function of
a
given by the unique solution in
\left(s(a),\infty\right)
to

\displaystyle 1=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\gamma(a)-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right].

(3.3)

To see existence and uniqueness of the solution, note that for any
a>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))
s.t.
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\neq 0
,
\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\gamma-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right]
is a strictly decreasing (since
\overline{\Sigma}
is strictly positive) function of
\gamma
which approaches
\infty
as
\gamma\searrow s(a)
(see
(a)
in
Equation

2.6
) and approaches
0
as
\gamma\nearrow\infty
.
If
\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]=0
, the solution
\gamma(a)=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]>0
is obviously unique.

Next, using
\psi
and
\varphi
, we define two parameters
a^{*},a^{\circ}
that govern the validity of our spectral characterization.
It can be shown (see
Lemma

E.3
) that
\psi
is differentiable and has at least one critical point.
Let
a^{\circ}>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))
be the largest solution to

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(a^{\circ})

\displaystyle=0.

(3.4)

We then define
\zeta\colon(\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})),\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
by flattening
\psi
to the left of
a^{\circ}
:

\displaystyle\zeta(a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\psi(\max\{a,a^{\circ}\}).

(3.5)

Finally, let
a^{*}
be the largest solution in
(\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})),\infty)
to the following equation:

\displaystyle\zeta(a^{*})=\varphi(a^{*}).

(3.6)

Proposition

D.1
shows that such a solution must exist.
The functions
\varphi,\psi,\zeta
are plotted in
Figure

1
for two examples of covariance matrix
\Sigma
.

(a)

The Toeplitz case with
\delta=0.2
.

(b)

The circulant case with
\delta=1.5
.

Figure 1
:
Plots of the functions
\varphi,\psi,\zeta\colon\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}^{*}(\overline{Y}))\to\infty
defined in

Equations

3.2
and
3.5

with
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
obtained by truncating the optimal preprocessing and
\overline{\Sigma}
given by the Toeplitz or circulant matrices
(see
Section

4.1
for details).

Then, the limits of the top two eigenvalues of
D
are given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\coloneqq\zeta(a^{*}),\quad\lambda_{2}\coloneqq\zeta(a^{\circ}),

(3.7)

and the asymptotic overlap admits the following explicit expression:

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\frac{(1-w_{2})\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\gamma(a^{*})-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right]^{2}}{(1-w_{2})\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\left(\gamma(a^{*})-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right]+w_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\left(\gamma(a^{*})-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right]}\right)^{1/2},

(3.8)

where the ancillary parameters
w_{1},w_{2}
are given by:

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\overline{G}^{2}-1\right)\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right)^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\gamma(a^{*})-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}~+\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right)^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{3}}{\left(\gamma(a^{*})-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right],

(3.9)

\displaystyle w_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right)^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\left(\gamma(a^{*})-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right].

(3.10)

We note that, given
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
,
\eta
is well-defined as the fraction under the square root is strictly positive.
This is because
(i)
all three expectations in
Equation

3.8
are positive (
\overline{\Sigma}>0
in
Item

(A2)
and
\gamma(a^{*})>s(a^{*})
);
(ii)

w_{1}>0
(see
Proposition

G.1
);
(iii)

1-w_{2}>0
if
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
(see
Item

3
of
Proposition

D.6
).

We are now ready to state our main result, whose proof is given in
Section

5
, with several details deferred to

Appendix

A
.

Theorem 3.1

(Performance characterization of spectral estimator)
.

Consider the setting of
Section

2
and let
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
,
(A5)
,
(A6)
and
(A7)
hold.
Suppose
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
.
Then, the top two eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(D),\lambda_{2}(D)
of
D
satisfy
3
3

3

For a symmetric matrix
M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times p}
, we write its (real) eigenvalues as
\lambda_{1}(M)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}(M)
and the associated eigenvectors (normalized to have unit
\ell_{2}
-norm) as
v_{1}(M),\cdots,v_{p}(M)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}
.

\displaystyle\plim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1},\qquad\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)=\lambda_{2}\quad\text{almost surely},

(3.11)

and
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
, where
\plim
denotes the limit in probability.
Furthermore, the limiting overlap between the top eigenvector
v_{1}(D)
and
\beta^{*}
equals

\displaystyle\plim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle v_{1}(D),\beta^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\eta>0.

(3.12)

Remark 3.1

(Uniqueness of
a^{*}
)
.

Recall that the parameter
a^{*}
is the largest solution in
(\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})),\infty)
to
Equation

3.6
.
With additional assumptions, we can show that
Equation

3.6
admits a unique solution; see
Proposition

D.3
for details.
We expect that the additional assumptions can be removed and the solution to
Equation

3.6
in
(\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})),\infty)
always exists and is unique.

Remark 3.2

(Consistency with isotropic covariance)
.

We note that, by setting
\Sigma=I_{d}
, we recover the existing result on i.i.d. Gaussian designs (i.e., Lemma 2 in
[
MM19
]
).

Remark 3.3

(Removing
Items

(A6)
and
(A7)
)
.

Item

(A6)
requires
\mathrm{law}(\overline{\Sigma})
to have sufficiently slow decay on both the left and right edges, and
Item

(A7)
requires such behaviour on the right edge of
\mathrm{law}({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))
.
However, both assumptions can be removed at the cost of a vanishing perturbation of
\overline{\Sigma},{\mathcal{T}}
around their edges in the definitions of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\eta
in
Equations

3.7
and
3.8
.
The perturbed quantities, denoted by
\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
, are guaranteed to satisfy both assumptions.
Hence,
Theorem

3.1
ensures that the high-dimensional limits of the top two eigenvalues and of the overlap for the perturbed matrix
D^{\prime}
are given by
\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
, respectively.
An application of the Davis–Kahan theorem
[
DK70
]
shows that, as the perturbation vanishes, the top two eigenvalues and overlap obtained with
D^{\prime}
coincide with those given by the unperturbed matrix
D
.
Furthermore, since
\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
are continuous with respect to the perturbation, their limits as the perturbation vanishes exist.
Therefore, the latter limits must equal the high-dimensional limits of the top two eigenvalues and overlap given by the original
D
. The formal argument is deferred to
Appendix

C
, and by a similar argument,
Items

(A6)
and
(A7)
in
Theorem

3.2
below can be removed as well.

Remark 3.4

(Phase transition)
.

Our characterization of the outlier eigenvalue and the overlap is valid given an explicit and checkable condition
a^{\circ}>a^{*}
not depending on the data
(y,X)
.
Informally, it guarantees that the aspect ratio
\delta
exceeds a certain threshold which leads to a spike in
D
. We conjecture that this condition is in fact necessary, in the sense that otherwise the spectral estimator fails to achieve a positive limiting overlap and the top eigenvalue sticks to the bulk of the spectrum of
D
.
It is easy to verify that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
and
\eta=0
precisely when
a^{*}=a^{\circ}
, indicating a continuous phase transition at the conjectured threshold.

3.1
Optimal spectral methods for general Gaussian designs

Theorem

3.1
holds for an arbitrary function
{\mathcal{T}}
subject to mild regularity conditions. This enables the optimization of
{\mathcal{T}}
to minimize the spectral threshold, i.e., the smallest
\delta
s.t.
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
. The result on the optimization of the pre-processing function is stated below and proved in
Appendix

B
.

Theorem 3.2

(Optimal spectral threshold)
.

Consider the setting of
Section

2
, let
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
and
(A6)
hold, and let
{\mathscr{T}}
be the set of functions
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying
Items

(A5)
and
(A7)
.
Then the following two statements hold.

1.

There exists
{\mathcal{T}}\in{\mathscr{T}}
such that
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
holds if

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle>\Delta(\delta):=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}^{2}\right]}\left(\int_{\supp(\overline{Y})}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\overline{G}^{2}-1\right)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\right]}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1},

(3.13)

with
p(\overline{Y}\,|\,\overline{G})
the conditional density of
\overline{Y}=q(\overline{G},\overline{\varepsilon})
given
\overline{G}
, determined via the joint distribution in
Equation

2.3
.
In this case, if

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(y)=1-\left(\sqrt{\frac{\Delta(\delta)}{\delta}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\overline{G}^{2}\right)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\right]}+1-\sqrt{\frac{\Delta(\delta)}{\delta}}\right)^{-1}

(3.14)

is pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order,
then the spectral estimator using the preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
achieves strictly positive limiting overlap.

2.

Conversely, suppose that the function
\varphi
defined in
Equation

3.2
is strictly decreasing for every
{\mathcal{T}}\in{\mathscr{T}}
. If there exists
{\mathcal{T}}\in{\mathscr{T}}
such that
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
, then
\delta
satisfies
Equation

3.13
.

Remark 3.5

(Mild dependence of
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
on
\overline{\Sigma}
)
.

The optimal function
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
in
Equation

3.14
depends on
\overline{\Sigma}
only through its first moment, or equivalently it depends on
\Sigma
only through its normalized trace. We highlight that approximating
\frac{1}{d}\tr(\Sigma)
from the data is significantly easier than approximating the whole matrix
\Sigma
. In fact,
\frac{1}{d}\tr(\Sigma)
can be estimated consistently via the plugin estimator
\frac{1}{d}\tr(X^{\top}X)
. Specifically, achieving a root mean square error of
\varsigma
only requires
n={\mathcal{O}}(\varsigma^{-2})
, which is trivially satisfied by
Item

(A4)
. In contrast, the sample complexity needed to estimate
\Sigma
with sufficient accuracy may be larger than that required by the spectral estimator itself. Specifically,
achieving an error of
\varsigma
in spectral norm for the estimation of
\Sigma
via the sufficient statistic
X^{\top}X
requires
n=\Theta(d\varsigma^{-2})
; see
[
PW24
, Exercise VI.15]
,
[
Wu17
, Section 24.2]
. Note that, to estimate
\Sigma
,
n
scales linearly with
d
and the proportionality constant may be larger than the critical value of
\delta
in the right-hand side of
Equation

3.13
; instead, to estimate
\frac{1}{d}\tr(\Sigma)
,
n
does not depend on
d
and, hence, the estimate is consistent for all
\delta>0
.

Remark 3.6

(Sufficient condition for
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
being pseudo-Lipschitz)
.

The assumption in
Theorem

3.2
that
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
is pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order is satisfied by models that contain an additive component of Gaussian noise (regardless of the variance of the Gaussian noise). This requirement is mild, and common in the related literature, see e.g.
[
BKM+19
]
.
Specifically, consider the GLM
y=\widetilde{q}(X\beta^{*},\varepsilon^{\prime})+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
, where
\widetilde{q}(X\beta^{*},\varepsilon^{\prime})
satisfies
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
and
(A6)
and is independent of
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{n},\sigma^{2}I_{n})
(for some
\sigma>0
).
Then, one can verify that
\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\overline{G}^{2}\right)\right]/\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\right]
, and hence
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(y)
, is pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order.

Remark 3.7

(Monotonicity of
\varphi
)
.

The second part of
Theorem

3.2
assumes the monotonicity of
\varphi
.
One readily checks that this holds when
\overline{\Sigma}=1
(i.e.,
\Sigma=I_{d}
).
Furthermore, in
Section

D.1
, we prove that
\varphi
is strictly decreasing for non-negative
{\mathcal{T}}
(
Proposition

D.2
) and give numerical evidence that the same result holds for general
{\mathcal{T}}
(
Remark

D.1
).

3.2
Optimal spectral methods for rotationally invariant designs

Equation

3.13
can be interpreted as giving the optimal spectral threshold, i.e., the minimal
\delta
above which positive overlap is achievable by some spectral estimator.
Furthermore, this threshold is attained by
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
in
Equation

3.14
. As
\delta
gets close to the spectral threshold
\Delta(\delta)
,
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
approaches the following function (obtained by replacing
\sqrt{\Delta(\delta)/\delta}
in
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
with
1
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{\star}(y)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[p(y\,|\,\overline{G})\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\overline{G}^{2}\right)\right]}.

(3.15)

When
\Sigma=I_{d}
,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\star}
minimizes the spectral threshold
[
MM19
]
and maximizes the overlap for any
\delta
above that threshold
[
LAL19
]
. Supported by evidence from statistical physics,
[
MKLZ22
, Conjecture 2]
conjectures the optimality to hold for the more general ensemble of right rotationally invariant designs. Although our design
X
is only left rotationally invariant, if the unknown parameter is Gaussian (
\beta^{*}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{d},I_{d})
) or uniform on the sphere (
\beta^{*}\sim\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
), the model in
Equation

1.1
is equivalent to one with a design that is also right rotationally invariant.
Therefore,
Theorem

3.2
proves
[
MKLZ22
, Conjecture 2]
for a class of spectral distributions of
X
– specifically, those given by the multiplicative free convolution of the Marchenko-Pastur law with a measure compactly supported on
(0,\infty)
.
Formally, with the following two assumptions in place of
Items

(A1)
and
(A2)
,
Theorem

3.2
implies
Corollary

3.3
.

(A8)

\beta^{*}\sim\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
or
\beta^{*}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{d},I_{d})
.

(A9)

X=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
can be written as
X=BQ^{\top}
, with the rows of
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
satisfying
Item

(A2)
and
Q\sim\mathrm{Haar}({\mathbb{O}}(d))
independent of everything else, where
{\mathbb{O}}(d)
is the orthogonal group in dimension
d
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Consider the setting of
Section

2
and let
Items

(A8)
,
(A9)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
and
(A6)
hold.
Then, the conclusions of
Theorem

3.2
hold.

Proof.

By
Item

(A9)
,
X=\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2}Q^{\top}
,
where
\widetilde{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
has i.i.d.
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/n)
entries and
\Sigma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is a covariance matrix satisfying
Item

(A2)
.
Let

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=X^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(X\beta^{*},\varepsilon)))X=Q\Sigma^{1/2}\widetilde{B}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2}Q^{\top}\beta^{*},\varepsilon)))\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2}Q^{\top},

\displaystyle\widehat{D}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{1/2}\widetilde{B}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2}Q^{\top}\beta^{*},\varepsilon)))\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}

\displaystyle=\Sigma^{1/2}\widetilde{B}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(q(\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2}\beta^{*},\varepsilon)))\widetilde{B}\Sigma^{1/2}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\left\langle v_{1}(D),\beta^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\left\langle Qv_{1}(\widehat{D}),\beta^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}}=\frac{\left|\left\langle v_{1}(\widehat{D}),Q^{\top}\beta^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|Q^{\top}\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\frac{\left|\left\langle v_{1}(\widetilde{D}),\beta^{*}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|\beta^{*}\right\|_{2}}.

(3.16)

The first equality uses that, if
(\lambda,v)
is an eigenpair of
D
, then
(\lambda,Qv)
is an eigenpair of
QDQ^{\top}
for
Q\in{\mathbb{O}}(d)
.
The second equality holds as
Q
is orthogonal.
The third passage follows since by
Item

(A8)
,
\beta^{*}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}Q^{\top}\beta^{*}
for
Q\in\mathrm{Haar}({\mathbb{O}}(d))
independent of
\beta^{*}
.
Now
Theorem

3.2
applies to the rightmost side of
Equation

3.16
,
which completes the proof.
∎

4
Numerical experiments

We consider noiseless phase retrieval (
y_{i}=|\langle x_{i},\beta^{*}\rangle|
), and evaluate the performance of the spectral estimator with different preprocessing functions.

4.1
Synthetic data

For all the synthetic experiments, we take the parameter
\beta^{*}\sim\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
and
d=2000
. We plot the overlap between the spectral estimator and
\beta^{*}
, as a function of the aspect ratio
\delta
.
Each value is computed from
10
i.i.d. trials,
the error bar is at
1
standard deviation, and the corresponding theoretical predictions are continuous lines with the same color.
We consider three types of covariance matrix
\Sigma
:
(i)
Toeplitz covariance,
\Sigma_{i,j}=\rho^{|i-j|}
for
1\leq i,j\leq d
with
\rho=0.9
, as considered in
[
ZZ14
, Section 4]
and
[
JM18
, Section 5.3]
.

(ii)
Circulant covariance,
\Sigma_{i,j}=c_{0}
for
i=j
,
\Sigma_{i,j}=c_{1}
for
i+1\leq j\leq i+\ell
and
i+d-\ell\leq j\leq i+d-1
,
\Sigma_{i,j}=0
otherwise, with
c_{0}=1,c_{1}=0.1,\ell=17
, as considered in
[
JM14b
, Section F]
and
[
JM14a
, Section 5.1]
.

(iii)
Identity covariance,
\Sigma=I_{d}
.

Figure 2
:
Overlap of spectral estimators with different preprocessing functions for noiseless phase retrieval when the covariate vectors are independent zero-mean Gaussians with Toeplitz (top row) and circulant (bottom row) covariance.

We compare spectral estimators using different preprocessing functions:
(i)
The optimal choice in
Equation

3.14
with truncation, i.e.,
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(y)=\max\left\{1-\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]/(\delta y^{2}),-K_{*}\right\}
with
K_{*}=10
.
The truncation ensures that the preprocessing is bounded as required by our theory and, by taking
K_{*}
sufficiently large, it does not affect performance.
(ii)
The trimming scheme
[
CC17
]
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{trim}}(y)=\delta y^{2}/\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]\mathds{1}{\left\{\sqrt{\delta}\left|y\right|/\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\leq K_{\mathrm{trim}}\right\}}
with
K_{\mathrm{trim}}=\sqrt{7}
.

(iii)
The subset scheme
[
WGE18
]
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{subset}}(y)=\mathds{1}{\left\{\sqrt{\delta}\left|y\right|/\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\geq K_{\mathrm{subset}}\right\}}
with
K_{\mathrm{subset}}=\sqrt{2}
. The values of both
K_{\mathrm{trim}}
and
K_{\mathrm{subset}}
are taken from
[
MM19
, Section 7.1]
where they are optimized to yield the smallest spectral threshold for
\Sigma=I_{d}
.
(iv)
The identity function with truncation, i.e.,
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{id}}(y)\coloneqq\min\left\{\max\left\{\sqrt{\delta}y/\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]},-K_{\mathrm{id}}\right\},K_{\mathrm{id}}\right\}
,
with
K_{\mathrm{id}}
equal to
3.5
and
3
for circulant and Toeplitz covariances, respectively.
Empirically, the performance under these choices of
K_{\mathrm{id}}
does not differ much from avoiding the truncation, i.e.,
K_{\mathrm{{id}}}=\infty
.

We also compare the performance with a
whitened
spectral estimator, which requires knowledge of the covariance
\Sigma
. The whitened spectral estimator is given by

\displaystyle\beta^{\mathrm{spec}}_{\smalltriangleup}\coloneqq\Sigma^{-1/2}v_{1}(D_{\smalltriangleup}),

(4.1)

where
D_{\smalltriangleup}\coloneqq(X\Sigma^{-1/2})^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{T}}(y))(X\Sigma^{-1/2})
.
This estimator uses
\Sigma
to whiten
X
and computes the principal eigenvector of
D_{\smalltriangleup}
obtained via the decorrelated covariates
X\Sigma^{-1/2}
.
As the eigenvector can be thought of as an estimate of
\Sigma^{1/2}\beta^{*}
, it is left-multiplied by
\Sigma^{-1/2}
to produce an estimate of
\beta^{*}
. Formal results and proofs on
\beta^{\mathrm{spec}}_{\smalltriangleup}
are deferred to
Appendix

F
.

Figure

2
shows that our proposed optimal spectral estimator significantly outperforms the trimming/subset schemes for both Toeplitz (top) and circular (bottom) covariances. Furthermore, in a large interval of
\delta
, the performance of the whitened spectral estimator in
Equation

4.1
(which requires
\Sigma
) is significantly worse than that of the standard spectral estimator (which does not require
\Sigma
), even though optimal preprocessing functions are employed for both.

In
Figure

3
, the plots for Toeplitz, circulant and identity covariance are superimposed.
An interesting observation is that there is no universally best covariance structure, even if the optimal preprocessing function with respect to the corresponding covariance is adopted.

Figure 3
:
Overlap of spectral estimators with optimal preprocessing function for noiseless phase retrieval when the covariate vectors are independent zero-mean Gaussians with Toeplitz/circulant/identity covariance.
The right three panels respectively zoom into regimes where
\delta
takes low, moderate and high values to demonstrate that in this particular setting, any one of the three types of covariance structures can attain the highest overlap.

4.2
Real data

We also demonstrate the advantage of the optimal preprocessing given by our theory for datasets popular in quantitative genetics and computational imaging.

Specifically, the design matrices for the first two plots of
Figure

4
are obtained from two GTEx datasets “skin sun exposed lower leg” (
56200\times 701
) and “muscle skeletal” (
56200\times 803
)
[
LTS+13
]
.
These matrices record gene counts and therefore contain non-negative entries.
We preprocess them as follows:
(i)
remove all-
0
rows,

(ii)
build a matrix by sequentially including each row only if it has an overlap smaller than
0.3
with all existing rows, and

(iii)
center and normalize each row such that it has zero mean and unit variance. All these operations are typical in genetic studies, see e.g. the widely used toolset PLINK
[
CCT+15
]
. The unknown parameter vector is given by
\beta^{*}\sim\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
for
d\in\{701,803\}
.
For each
\delta
, the design matrix is formed by the first
\left\lfloor d\delta\right\rfloor
rows of the above preprocessed matrix.
The value of overlap for each
\delta
is computed from
100
i.i.d. trials where the randomness is only over
\beta^{*}
, and the error bar is reported at
1
standard deviation.
The truncation levels for different preprocessing functions are chosen as follows:
for
{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
, we set
K_{*}=100
;
for
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{trim}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{subset}}
, for each
\delta
, we choose
K_{\mathrm{trim}}
and
K_{\mathrm{subset}}
in
\left\{0.25i:1\leq i\leq 40\right\}
to maximize the respective overlaps (averaged over
100
trials);
for
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{id}}
, we do not truncate, i.e.,
K_{\mathrm{id}}=\infty
. Despite the advantage due to the adaptive choice of the truncation level for the trimming/subset scheme, the preprocessing we propose still performs vastly better than all alternatives.

The design matrices for the last two plots of
Figure

4
follow a coded diffraction pattern
[
CLS15a
]
, i.e.,
X
is obtained by stacking in its rows the matrices
FD_{1}S,FD_{2}S,\ldots,FD_{\delta}S
. Here,
\delta\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
F\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is a Discrete Fourier Transform matrix,
S\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is diagonal containing i.i.d. uniformly random signs, and
D_{1},D_{2},\cdots,D_{\delta}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d\times d}
are diagonal with elements following one of these two distributions:
(i)
uniform modulation,
(D_{\ell})_{i,i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\unif([-10,10])
, and

(ii)
octanary modulation
[
CLS15a
, Equation (1.9)]
,
(D_{\ell})_{i,i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathrm{law}(\overline{D})
with
\overline{D}=\overline{D}_{1}\overline{D}_{2}
,
\mathrm{law}(\overline{D}_{1})=\frac{1}{4}\left(\delta_{1}+\delta_{-1}+\delta_{-\mathrm{i}}+\delta_{\mathrm{i}}\right)
and
\mathrm{law}(\overline{D}_{2})=\frac{4}{5}\delta_{1/\sqrt{2}}+\frac{1}{5}\delta_{\sqrt{3}}
.
For fractional
\delta\in(0,\infty)
, we first construct a matrix of size
\left\lceil\delta\right\rceil d\times d
and then randomly subsample
\left\lfloor\delta d\right\rfloor-\left\lfloor\delta\right\rfloor d
rows from the last block
FD_{\left\lceil\delta\right\rceil}S
to obtain a design matrix of size
\left\lfloor\delta d\right\rfloor\times d
.

The parameter
\beta^{*}
in the last two plots of
Figure

4
is a
75\times 64
RGB image of the painting “Girl with a Pearl Earring”.
The
3
color bands give
3
matrices in
[0,256]^{75\times 64}
.
The parameter vectors
\beta_{\mathrm{R}}^{*},\beta_{\mathrm{G}}^{*},\beta_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
(with
d=75\times 64=4800
) are then obtained by vectorizing, centering, and normalizing each of these matrices.
For each
\delta
, we have
5
i.i.d. trials where the randomness is only over
X
.
In each trial, we compute
3
spectral estimators using the same
X
and observations
y_{\mathrm{R}},y_{\mathrm{G}},y_{\mathrm{B}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
generated from
\beta_{\mathrm{R}}^{*},\beta_{\mathrm{G}}^{*},\beta_{\mathrm{B}}^{*}
respectively.
We report the mean of
5\times 3=15
overlaps for each
\delta
with error bar at
1
standard deviation.
The truncation levels for different preprocessing functions are
K_{*}=10,K_{\mathrm{trim}}=\sqrt{7},K_{\mathrm{subset}}=\sqrt{2},K_{\mathrm{id}}=\infty
. For all datasets, our proposed preprocessing (optimal in red) outperforms previous heuristic choices (trimming
[
CC17
]
in black, subset
[
WGE18
]
in blue, and identity in green).

Figure 4
:
Overlap of spectral estimators for noiseless phase retrieval when the design matrix is obtained from two Genotype-Tissue Expression (GTEx) datasets (first two plots) and two coded diffraction patterns (CDP) (last two plots).

5
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

5.1
Overview of the argument

The outlier location and asymptotic overlap in
Theorem

3.1
are derived using
a variant of AMP for GLMs, known as
generalized approximate message passing (GAMP)

[
Ran11
]
,
[
FVRS22
, Section 4]
.
An instance of GAMP is specified by two sequences of denoising functions,
(g_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(f_{t+1})_{t\geq 0}
.
Starting with initialization
\widetilde{u}^{-1}=0_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and some
\widetilde{v}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, for
t\geq 0
the GAMP iterates are computed as:

\displaystyle\begin{split}u^{t}&=\widetilde{X}\widetilde{v}^{t}-b_{t}\widetilde{u}^{t-1},\quad\widetilde{u}^{t}=g_{t}(u^{t};y),\quad c_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\div g_{t}(u^{t};y)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial g_{t}(u^{t};y)_{i}}{\partial u^{t}_{i}},\\
v^{t+1}&=\widetilde{X}^{\top}\widetilde{u}^{t}-c_{t}\widetilde{v}^{t},\quad\widetilde{v}^{t+1}=f_{t+1}(v^{t+1}),\quad b_{t+1}=\frac{1}{n}\div f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})_{i}}{\partial v^{t+1}_{i}},\end{split}

(5.1)

where we recall
\widetilde{X}=X\Sigma^{-1/2}
. To handle
\Sigma\neq I_{d}
, the denoising functions
g_{t}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
f_{t+1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
need to be
non-separable
, i.e., they cannot be decomposed in terms of functions acting component-wise on the vector inputs.

AMP algorithms come with an associated deterministic scalar recursion called
state evolution
which describes the limiting distribution (as
d\to\infty
) of the AMP iterates
u^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
v^{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
using a collection of Gaussian vectors.
The covariance structure of these Gaussians admits a succinct representation which can be recursively tracked via the state evolution. The state evolution result for GAMP with non-separable denoisers is not immediately available – we prove it by reducing such a GAMP to a general family of abstract AMP algorithms introduced in
[
GB23
]
for which a state evolution has been established. This is detailed in
Section

5.2
.

The key idea is to design a GAMP algorithm that simulates the power iteration
v^{t+1}=Dv^{t}/\left\|Dv^{t}\right\|_{2}
, via a careful choice of denoising functions
g_{t}
and
f_{t+1}
. To this end, we set

\displaystyle g_{t}(u^{t};y)

\displaystyle=Fu^{t},\quad t\geq 0,

(5.2)

where
F=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{F}}(y))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
, and the functions
\mathcal{F},(f_{t+1})_{t\geq 0}
are specified later. With this choice for
g_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle c_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{F}}(y_{i})\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{Y})\right]\eqqcolon c,\quad t\geq 0,

(5.3)

where we recall that
\overline{Y}
is defined in
Equation

2.3
.
Thus, the GAMP iteration, with
c_{t}
replaced with its high-dimensional limit, becomes

\displaystyle u^{t}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}f_{t}(v^{t})-b_{t}Fu^{t-1},\quad v^{t+1}=\widetilde{X}^{\top}Fu^{t}-cf_{t}(v^{t}).

We show in
Section

5.5
that
u^{t},v^{t+1},b_{t},f_{t+1}
converge in probability as
t\to\infty
, i.e., there exist
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},b\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|u^{t}-u\right\|_{2}&=0,\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left\|v^{t+1}-v\right\|_{2}=0,\\
\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\left|b_{t}-b\right|&=0,\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left\|f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})-f(v)\right\|_{2}=0.\end{split}

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}f(v)-bFu,\quad v=\widetilde{X}^{\top}Fu-cf(v).

The first equation for
u
implies

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\left(I_{n}+bF\right)^{-1}\widetilde{X}f(v).

Substituting this into the equation for
v
and multiplying both sides by
\Sigma^{1/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma^{1/2}\left(v+cf(v)\right)=\Sigma^{1/2}\widetilde{X}^{\top}F\left(I_{n}+bF\right)^{-1}\widetilde{X}\Sigma^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}f(v).

(5.4)

At this point, we consider the following choice of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
f
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)}{a-b{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)},\qquad f(v)=(\gamma I_{d}-c\Sigma)^{-1}\Sigma v,

(5.5)

for some
a,\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
to be specified. Then,
Equation

5.4
becomes

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1/2}f(v)=\frac{1}{a\gamma}\Sigma^{1/2}\widetilde{X}^{\top}T\widetilde{X}\Sigma^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}f(v)=\frac{1}{a\gamma}D\Sigma^{-1/2}f(v),

which is an eigenequation of
D
with eigenvalue
a\gamma\eqqcolon\lambda_{1}
and eigenvector (possibly scaled by a constant)

\Sigma^{-1/2}f(v)=\Sigma^{-1/2}(\gamma I_{d}-c\Sigma)^{-1}\Sigma v
.
Assuming a spectral gap, we expect that
\lambda_{1}
equals the limiting value of
\lambda_{1}(D)
and
\Sigma^{-1/2}f(v)
is asymptotically aligned with
v_{1}(D)
.

It remains to pick
a,\gamma
which are in principle free parameters. Our choice is motivated by the fixed points of state evolution characterized in
Section

5.3
, and it simplifies the derivations. Specifically, the limiting Onsager coefficient is given by

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial f(v)_{i}}{\partial v_{i}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{d}((\gamma I_{d}-c\Sigma)^{-1}\Sigma)_{i,i}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\gamma-c\overline{\Sigma}}\right].

Then, we choose
(a,\gamma)
to satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\|f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})\right\|_{2}^{2}=1,\qquad b=1.

(5.6)

The constraint on
\left\|f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})\right\|_{2}^{2}
normalizes the GAMP iterate so that, as
t
grows, its norm does not blow up or vanish. Using state evolution and the characterization of its fixed points, we can show that the conditions
Equation

5.6
be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}1&=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\overline{G}^{2}-1\right)\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\gamma-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right],\\
1&=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}}{\gamma-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}}\right].\end{split}

(5.7)

Proposition

D.4
shows that in the presence of a spectral gap,
Equation

5.7
is equivalent to
\zeta(a)=\varphi(a)
, with
\varphi,\zeta
defined in
Equations

3.2
and
3.5
. Thus, from
Equation

3.6
, we have that
(a,\gamma)=(a^{*},\gamma(a^{*}))
.

With the above choice of denoisers, the GAMP iteration can be expressed as

\displaystyle\widehat{v}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{a^{*}\gamma(a^{*})}\widehat{v}^{t}+\widehat{e}^{t},

(5.8)

for some auxiliary iterate
\widehat{v}^{t+1}
and error term
\widehat{e}^{t}
. We show in
Section

A.5
that
\widehat{e}^{t}
asymptotically vanishes as
t
grows. Now, if
\widehat{e}^{t}
is zero,
Equation

5.8
is exactly a power iteration for
M\coloneqq(a^{*}\gamma(a^{*}))^{-1}D
. The convergence of this power iteration to the leading eigenvector of
M
(or, equivalently, of
D
) crucially relies on the existence of a spectral gap, i.e., on the fact that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)>\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)
.

To pinpoint when a spectral gap exists, we establish the limiting value of
\lambda_{2}(D)
. In
Section

5.4
, we prove that
\lambda_{2}(D)
converges to
\lambda_{2}\coloneqq a^{\circ}\gamma(a^{\circ})
, where
a^{\circ}
is given as in
Equation

3.4
. This is obtained by interlacing the eigenvalues of
D
with those of a “decoupled” matrix
\widehat{D}
in which
X
is replaced with an i.i.d. copy
\widehat{X}
independent of
T
. The support of the limiting spectral distribution of
\widehat{D}
is characterized in
[
CH14
, Section 3]
, when
T
is positive semi-definite. By extending this analysis, we deduce the desired characterization of
\lambda_{2}
. One technical challenge is that, when
T
is not positive semi-definite, the roles of
\Sigma
and
T
are not interchangeable in determining
\lambda_{2}
, whereas in
[
CH14
]
this symmetry simplifies the arguments.

Given the normalization in
Equation

5.6
, the largest eigenvalue of
M
converges to
1
and, thus,
\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)=\lambda_{1}:=a^{*}\gamma(a^{*})
. Hence, the criticality condition for the existence of a spectral gap reads
a^{*}\gamma(a^{*})>a^{\circ}\gamma(a^{\circ})
.
This is equivalent to
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
, as adopted in
Theorem

3.1
, by the monotonicity properties of the function
\psi(a)=a\gamma(a)
in
Equation

3.2
(see
Lemma

E.1
).

To formalize the above reasoning, assume
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
and execute
Equation

5.8
for
t^{\prime}
steps to amplify the spectral gap:

\displaystyle\widehat{v}^{t+t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\approx M^{t^{\prime}}\widehat{v}^{t},

(5.9)

where the error terms can be neglected by taking
t
sufficiently large (and also much larger than
t^{\prime}
).
Now, we look at the rescaled norms
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}
of both sides of
Equation

5.9
. Due to the GAMP state evolution, the rescaled norm of the left-hand side
\left\|\widehat{v}^{t+t^{\prime}}\right\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}
can be accurately determined in the high-dimensional limit.
Furthermore, it converges to an explicit strictly positive constant in the large
t
limit, by convergence of state evolution. Thus, inspecting the right-hand side of
Equation

5.9
allows us to conclude that
\lambda_{1}(M)
must be
1
in the high-dimensional limit.
Indeed, if that’s not the case,
\left\|M^{t^{\prime}}\widehat{v}^{t}\right\|_{2}/\sqrt{d}
would be either amplified or shrunk geometrically as
t^{\prime}
grows, violating the equality in
Equation

5.9
.
At this point, we
have
\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)=\lambda_{1}
,
\lim\limits_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)=\lambda_{2}
and that
\widehat{v}^{t}
is asymptotically
aligned
with the top eigenvector
v_{1}(D)
, provided
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
.
Then, the limiting overlap between
\beta^{*}
and
v_{1}(D)
is the same as that between
\beta^{*}
and
\widehat{v}^{t}
, which is again derived using state evolution.

The rest of this section is organized as follows:
Section

5.2
presents the state evolution of GAMP with non-separable denoisers,
Section

5.3
establishes its fixed points when GAMP simulates a power iteration,
Section

5.4
characterizes the right edge of the bulk of
D
, and
Section

5.5
puts everything together concluding the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

5.2
State evolution of GAMP with non-separable denoisers

To precisely state the state evolution result for GAMP, we require the notion of pseudo-Lipschitz functions with matrix-valued inputs and outputs.

Definition 5.1

(Pseudo-Lipschitz functions)
.

A function
h\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times m}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell\times m}
is called
pseudo-Lipschitz of order
j
if there exists
L
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell}}\left\|h(x)-h(y)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{\sqrt{k}}\left\|x-y\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\left[1+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\left\|x\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right)^{j-1}+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\left\|y\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right)^{j-1}\right],

(5.10)

for every
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times m}
.

We will consider sequences of functions
h_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{i}\times m}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{i}\times m}
indexed by
i\to\infty
though the index
i
is often not written explicitly.
A sequence of functions
(h_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{i}\times m}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{i}\times m})_{i\geq 1}
is called
uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz of order
j
if there exists a constant
L
such that for every
i\geq 1
,
Equation

5.10
holds.
Note that
L
is a constant as
i\to\infty
.

Define the random vectors

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{B}^{*}&\sim P^{\otimes d},\qquad\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathfrak{B}^{*},\qquad(G,\varepsilon)\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(0_{n},\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]I_{n}\right)\otimes P_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes n},\qquad Y=q(G,\varepsilon).\end{split}

(5.11)

If
\beta^{*}\sim\unif(\sqrt{d}\,{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
,
P
should be taken to be
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.

We further impose the following assumptions which guarantee the existence and finiteness of various state evolution parameters.

(A10)

The initializer
\widetilde{v}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is independent of
\widetilde{X}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\plim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left\|\widetilde{v}^{0}\right\|_{2}

(5.12)

exists and is finite.
There exists a uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz function
f_{0}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of order
1
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{0}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}),f_{0}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\plim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\|\widetilde{v}^{0}\right\|_{2}^{2},

and for every uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz
h\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of finite order,

\displaystyle\plim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\langle\widetilde{v}^{0},h(\widetilde{\beta}^{*})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{0}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}),h(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*})\right\rangle\right];

(5.13)

in particular, limits on both sides of the above two displayed equations exist and are finite.
Here, we have set
\widetilde{\beta}^{*}=\Sigma^{1/2}\beta^{*}
and we recall that
\beta^{*}\sim P^{\otimes d}
from
Item

(A1)
.
Let
\widetilde{\chi}\in{\mathbb{R}},\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.
For any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{0}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}),f_{t+1}\left(\widetilde{\chi}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}+\widetilde{\sigma}_{V}\widetilde{W}_{V}\right)\right\rangle\right]

exists and is finite, where
\widetilde{W}_{V}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{d},I_{d})
is independent of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}
.

(A11)

Let
\widetilde{\nu}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
T\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}
be positive definite.
For
s,t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{s+1}(\widetilde{\nu}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}+\widetilde{N}),f_{t+1}(\widetilde{\nu}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}+\widetilde{N}^{\prime})\right\rangle\right]

exists and is finite, where
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},(\widetilde{N},\widetilde{N}^{\prime}))\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{d},\Sigma)\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0_{2d},T\otimes I_{d})
.
Let
\widetilde{\mu}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, and
S\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}
be positive definite.
For any
s,t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle g_{s}(\widetilde{G}+\widetilde{M};Y),g_{t}(\widetilde{G}+\widetilde{M}^{\prime};Y)\right\rangle\right],\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(\div_{g}g_{t}(u,q(g,e))\right)\right|_{u=\widetilde{G}+\widetilde{M},g=\widetilde{G},e=\varepsilon}\right]

exist and are finite, where
(\widetilde{G},\varepsilon,\widetilde{M},\widetilde{M}^{\prime})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{n},\widetilde{\mu}^{2}I_{n})\otimes P_{\varepsilon}^{\otimes n}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0_{2n},S\otimes I_{n})
and
Y=q(\widetilde{G},\varepsilon)
.

The state evolution result – formally stated below – asserts that, for each
t\geq 0
, in the large
n
limit, the joint distributions of the AMP iterates
(\widetilde{\beta}^{*},v^{1},v^{2},\cdots,v^{t+1})
and
(g=X\beta^{*},u^{0},u^{1},\cdots,u^{t})
converge to the laws of
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},V_{1},V_{2},\cdots,V_{t+1})
and
(G,U_{0},U_{1},\cdots,U_{t})
, respectively. For
t\geq 0
, the random vectors
U_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
V_{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
are defined as:

\displaystyle U_{t}=\mu_{t}G+\sigma_{U,t}W_{U,t},\qquad V_{t+1}=\chi_{t+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}+\sigma_{V,t+1}W_{V,t+1},

(5.14)

where
W_{U,t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{n},I_{n})
is independent of
(G,\varepsilon)
, and
W_{V,t+1}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{d},I_{d})
is independent of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}
. The constants
\mu_{t},\sigma_{U,t},\chi_{t+1},\sigma_{V,t+1}
are recursively defined, starting from

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},f_{0}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*})\right\rangle\right],\quad\sigma_{U,0}^{2}=\plim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\widetilde{v}^{0},\widetilde{v}^{0}\right\rangle-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}{\delta}\mu_{0}^{2}.

(5.15)

For
t\geq 0
, we have

\begin{split}\chi_{t+1}&=\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle G,g_{t}(U_{t};Y)\right\rangle\right]-\mu_{t}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\div_{U_{t}}g_{t}(U_{t};Y)\right],\\
\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle g_{t}(U_{t};Y),g_{t}(U_{t};Y)\right\rangle\right],\end{split}

(5.16)

and

\begin{split}\mu_{t+1}&=\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},f_{t}(V_{t+1})\right\rangle\right],\\
\sigma_{U,t+1}^{2}&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{t}(V_{t+1}),f_{t}(V_{t+1})\right\rangle\right]-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}{\delta}\mu_{t+1}^{2}.\end{split}

(5.17)

The two sequences of random vectors
(W_{U,t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(W_{V,t+1})_{t\geq 0}
, are each jointly Gaussian with the following laws:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\sigma_{U,0}W_{U,0}\\
\sigma_{U,1}W_{U,1}\\
\vdots\\
\sigma_{U,t}W_{U,t}\end{bmatrix}\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(0_{(t+1)n},\Phi_{t}\otimes I_{n}\right),\quad\begin{bmatrix}\sigma_{V,1}W_{V,1}\\
\sigma_{V,2}W_{V,2}\\
\vdots\\
\sigma_{V,t+1}W_{V,t+1}\end{bmatrix}\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(0_{(t+1)d},\Psi_{t}\otimes I_{d}\right),

(5.18)

where
\Phi_{t},\Psi_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(t+1)\times(t+1)}
are matrices with entries:

\displaystyle(\Phi_{t})_{1,1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\plim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left\langle\widetilde{v}^{0},\widetilde{v}^{0}\right\rangle-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}{\delta}\mu_{0}^{2},

\displaystyle(\Phi_{t})_{1,s+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{0}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*})-\mu_{0}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},f_{s}(V_{s})-\mu_{s}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\right\rangle\right],\quad\mbox{ for }\,\,1\leq s\leq t,

(5.19)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{t})_{r+1,s+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{r}(V_{r})-\mu_{r}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},f_{s}(V_{s})-\mu_{s}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\right\rangle\right],\quad\mbox{ for }\,\,1\leq r,s\leq t,

(5.20)

\displaystyle(\Psi_{t})_{r+1,s+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle g_{r}(U_{r};Y),g_{s}(U_{s};Y)\right\rangle\right],\quad\mbox{ for }\,\,0\leq r,s\leq t.

(5.21)

Note that for
r=s
,
(\Psi_{t})_{r+1,r+1}=\sigma_{V,r+1}^{2}
is consistent with
Equation

5.16
and

\displaystyle(\Phi_{t})_{r+1,r+1}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{r}(V_{r})-\mu_{r}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},f_{r}(V_{r})-\mu_{r}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{r}(V_{r}),f_{r}(V_{r})\right\rangle\right]-2\mu_{r}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{r}(V_{r}),\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\right\rangle\right]+\mu_{r}^{2}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle f_{r}(V_{r}),f_{r}(V_{r})\right\rangle\right]-2\mu_{r}^{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}{\delta}+\mu_{r}^{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}{\delta}=\sigma_{U,r}^{2}

is consistent with
Equation

5.17
, where
the last line above follows from the definition of
\mu_{r}
in
Equation

5.17
.
As
(G,W_{U,0},\cdots,W_{U,t})
are jointly Gaussian by
Equation

5.18
, their covariance structure (and therefore that of
(G,U_{0},\cdots,U_{t})
in view of
Equation

5.14
) is completely determined by the constants defined in

Equations

5.15
,
5.16
,
5.17
,
5.19
and
5.20
.
Similarly
(V_{1}-\chi_{1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},\cdots,V_{t+1}-\chi_{t+1}\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*})=(\sigma_{V,1}W_{V,1},\cdots,\sigma_{V,t+1}W_{V,t+1})
are jointly Gaussian by
Equations

5.14
and
5.18
, hence the covariance structure of
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},V_{1},\cdots,V_{t+1})
is completely determined by the constants in
Equations

5.16
and
5.21
.

We are now ready to present the state evolution result. Its proof, deferred to
Section

A.1
, reduces the GAMP iteration in
Equation

5.1
to a family of abstract AMP algorithms introduced in
[
GB23
]
for which a general state evolution result has been established.
In the abstract AMP algorithm, iterates are associated with the edges of a given directed graph, and the denoising functions are allowed to be non-separable, as needed in our case.

Proposition 5.1

(State evolution)
.

Consider the GLM in
Section

2.1
subject to
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
and
(A4)
and the GAMP iteration in
Equation

5.1
.
Let initializers
\widetilde{u}^{-1}=0_{n}
and
\widetilde{v}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
satisfy
Item

(A10)
.
For every
t\geq 0
,
let
(g_{t}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(f_{t+1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d})_{d\geq 1}
be uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz functions of finite constant order subject to
Item

(A11)
.
For any
t\geq 0
, let
(h_{1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n(t+2)}\to{\mathbb{R}})_{n\geq 1}
and
(h_{2}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d(t+2)}\to{\mathbb{R}})_{d\geq 1}
be two sequences of uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz test functions of finite order.
Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\plim_{n\to\infty}h_{1}(g,u^{0},u^{1},\cdots,u^{t})-\mathbb{E}\left[h_{1}(G,U_{0},U_{1},\cdots,U_{t})\right]&=0,\\
\plim_{d\to\infty}h_{2}(\widetilde{\beta}^{*},v^{1},v^{2},\cdots,v^{t+1})-\mathbb{E}\left[h_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*},V_{1},V_{2},\cdots,V_{t+1})\right]&=0,\end{split}

(5.22)

where
(U_{t},V_{t+1})_{t\geq 0}
are given in
Equation

5.14
.

5.3
GAMP as a power method and its fixed points

We now formalize the argument in
Section

5.1
. Recall the definition of
\overline{Y}
in
Equation

2.3
and
s(\cdot)
in
Equation

3.1
.
Let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\left\{(a,\gamma):a>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})),\gamma>s(a)\right\}

(5.23)

and
(a^{*},\gamma(a^{*}))\in{\mathcal{A}}
be defined through
Equation

3.6
, where the largest solution
a^{*}
is taken. For convenience, for the rest of the paper, we will use the shorthand

\displaystyle\gamma^{*}\coloneqq\gamma(a^{*}),\quad\gamma^{\circ}\coloneqq\gamma(a^{\circ}).

(5.24)

If
a^{*}>a^{\circ}
(where
a^{\circ}
is defined in
Equation

3.4
),
Proposition

D.4
shows that this pair of equations is equivalent to
Equation

5.7
.
Furthermore, let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\cdot)},\quad F=\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{F}}(y)),\quad c=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{Y})\right].

(5.25)

Let us initialize the iteration in
Equation

5.1
with
\widetilde{u}^{-1}=0_{n}
and
\widetilde{v}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
defined in
Equation

5.36
, and for subsequent iterates, set

\displaystyle g_{t}(u^{t};y)

\displaystyle=Fu^{t},\qquad f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})=(\gamma_{t+1}I_{d}-c\Sigma)^{-1}\Sigma v^{t+1},\quad t\geq 0.

(5.26)

Recall from
Item

(A5)
that
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is bounded and pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order.
Since
a^{*}>\sup\supp({\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y}))
,
{\mathcal{F}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is also bounded and pseudo-Lipschitz of finite order.
Therefore, for every
t\geq 0
,
(g_{t}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of uniformly pseudo-Lipschitz functions of finite order in both arguments.
The parameter
\gamma_{t+1}\in(s(a^{*}),\infty)
is s.t.

\displaystyle\plim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\|f_{t+1}(v^{t+1})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|f_{t+1}(V_{t+1})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]=1

(5.27)

for
t\geq 0
. The first equality above follows from the state evolution result in
Proposition

5.1
. For notational convenience, let

\displaystyle B_{t+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\gamma_{t+1}I_{d}-c\Sigma)^{-1}\Sigma.

(5.28)

Since
\gamma_{t+1}>s(a^{*})
and
\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}
is uniformly bounded by
Item

(A2)
,
\left\|B_{t+1}\right\|_{2}
is uniformly bounded.
Therefore for every
t\geq 0
,
(f_{t+1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d})_{d\geq 1}
is a sequence of pseudo-Lipschitz functions of order
1
.

With the above definitions, the Onsager coefficients become

\displaystyle c_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\tr(F),\quad b_{t+1}=\frac{d}{n}\tr(B_{t+1}),

(5.29)

for every
t\geq 0
.
Furthermore, the state evolution in
Equations

5.17
and
5.16
specializes to the following recursion

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu_{t}&=\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[(\widetilde{\mathfrak{B}}^{*})^{\top}B_{t}V_{t}\right],\\
\sigma_{U,t}^{2}&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[V_{t}^{\top}B_{t}^{\top}B_{t}V_{t}\right]-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}{\delta}\mu_{t}^{2},\\
\chi_{t+1}&=\frac{\delta}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{\Sigma}\right]}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{F}}(Y))U_{t}\right]-\mu_{t}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{Y})\right],\\
\sigma_{V,t+1}^{2}&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[U_{t}^{\top}\mathrm{diag}({\mathcal{F}}(Y))^{2}U_{t}\right].\end{split}

(5.30)

Let

\displaystyle z_{1}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{3}}{\left(\gamma^{*}-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right],\quad z_{2}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\overline{\Sigma}^{2}}{\left(\gamma^{*}-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}{a^{*}-{\mathcal{T}}(\overline{Y})}\right]\overline{\Sigma}\right)^{2}}\right].

(5.31)

Note that
z_{1},z_{2}>0
. Recalling
w_{1},w_{2}
from
Equations

3.9
and
3.10
,
define

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1-w_{2}}{(1-w_{2})z_{1}+w_{1}z_{2}}},\quad\sigma_{V}=\sqrt{\frac{w_{1}}{(1-w_{2})z_{1}+w_{1}z_{2}}},

(5.32)

\displaystyle\mu
